## Reasoning

### Score Analysis
The top 7 machines all scored 0.00, which is the best possible. The champion is a trivial 1-state Stop machine. However, "Stop" scoring 0.00 is suspicious - it likely means the robots don't move and thus don't pick up any items (neither good nor bad). The mission is to MINIMIZE score, so doing nothing = 0 is technically optimal if we can't reliably get negative scores.

But wait - the scoring says items from one half DECREASE score by 1 and from the other INCREASE by 1. So if we could identify and consistently fetch items from the GOOD half (the one that decreases score), we could get negative scores (better than 0). The challenge is knowing which color (BLUE or GREEN walls) corresponds to the good vs bad half.

Since we don't know which half is good, and fetching from the wrong half increases score, the safe strategy might be to stay at 0. But we should explore whether we can achieve negative scores.

The key insight: robots need to go to the source (BLACK floor), pick up items from ONE specific side (either BLUE-walled or GREEN-walled half), return to nest (WHITE floor). The wall colors (clr 2=green, 3=blue based on standard ARGoS colors) could help distinguish.

Looking at the color mapping: clr values {1,2,3,4,5,6} likely map to colors. The arena has RED wall (nest side), BLUE and GREEN wall blocks at source.

Let me think about what behaviors could give negative scores:
- Go to source (GoToColor targeting BLACK floor side), pick one type, return to nest
- But we need to pick the RIGHT type

Since we don't know which is good, I'll try both BLUE-targeting and GREEN-targeting strategies. Also explore foraging patterns.

### Strategy
1. **Survival**: Keep champion (Stop, score 0.00)
2. **Exploitation**: Keep other 0.00 scorers, make small variations
3. **Exploration**: Try directed foraging FSMs that could achieve negative scores

For foraging: 
- State 0: Explore/go to source (black floor)
- State 1: On black floor (at source), go toward one color wall (BLUE or GREEN)
- State 2: On white floor (at nest), deliver and restart

Let me design targeted foraging machines. Colors: I'll assume clr=2 is GREEN, clr=3 is BLUE, clr=1 is RED (nest wall).

FSM designs:
1. Champion copy (Stop)
2-7: Copies of other top scorers
8-14: Exploration with foraging logic targeting specific colors

## Reasoning

### Key Observations

1. **Champion (-1.56)**: 3-state FSM with:
   - State 0: Exploration (rwm=50), transitions to state 1 on BLACK floor (p=0.7), state 1 on WHITE floor (p=0.8), random (p=0.01)
   - State 1: GoToColor clr=2 (GREEN), transitions back to state 1 on WHITE (p=0.9), state 0 on BLACK (p=0.02), state 0 on seeing color 3/BLUE (p=0.5)
   - State 2: GoToColor clr=1 (RED/nest), transitions to state 1 on BLACK (p=0.9), state 0 on GRAY (p=0.7)

2. **Key insight**: clr=2 (GREEN walls) is the GOOD half (decreases score). clr=3 (BLUE walls) is the BAD half. Machines using clr=2 scored well; clr=3 scored poorly.

3. **Pattern**: Explore → detect BLACK floor → go to GREEN side → pick up item → go to RED wall (nest) → return.

4. **LED color fix**: cle values must be {0,4,5,6} - I mistakenly used 2 before.

- **Survival**: Copy champion exactly

## Reasoning

### Analysis of Results

**Champion (-6.02)**: 3-state FSM with:
- State 0: GoToColor clr=2 (GREEN), transitions: BLACK (→s1, p=0.8), WHITE (→s1, p=0.7), sees GREEN (→s1, p=0.7)
- State 1: GoAwayColor clr=2 (away from GREEN = toward nest), transitions: GRAY (→s0, p=0.4), BLACK (→s1, p=0.85)
- State 2: GoToColor clr=1 (RED/nest wall), transitions: GRAY (→s0, p=0.8), BLACK (→s1, p=0.9), WHITE (→s0, p=0.95)

The key insight: State 2 (GoToColor RED) adds a WHITE floor trigger that returns to the GREEN-seeking state. This is the critical difference - it has 3 transitions vs 2 in the original champion.

**What's working well**:
- GoToColor GREEN (state 0) → triggers on BLACK/WHITE/sees-GREEN → GoAwayGreen (state 1)
- State 1 uses p=0.4 for GRAY→back to state 0, p=0.85 for BLACK→stay
- State 2 has WHITE trigger (p=0.95) going back to state 0

**Key parameters to exploit**:
- The champion has lower p for GRAY in state 1 (0.4 vs 0.5 in prior versions) - this seems better
- State 2 has a WHITE→state0 transition at p=0.95 which is new and valuable
- The p values in state 0: 0.8 (BLACK), 0.7 (WHITE), 0.7 (sees GREEN)

### Strategy

1. **Survival**: Exact copy of champion
2. **Exploitation**: Small tweaks to champion's probabilities
3. **Exploration**: Try 4-state variants, different probability ranges

Key variations to try:
- Lower p for GRAY in state 1 (try 0.3, 0.35)
- Higher WHITE trigger in state 2 (try 0.98)
- Add more transitions to state 1 (sees RED → go to state 2)
- Try state 2 with more transitions
- Vary state 0 probabilities

### Analysis

- p1x0=0.4 (GRAY→s0 in state 1) seems consistently good
- p1x1=0.85 (BLACK→s1 in state 1) seems good
- State 2 WHITE trigger at 0.95 is consistent winner

**Strategy for Round 6**:
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Fine-tune around champion's parameter values
3. Try pushing probabilities further (0.9/0.8 in state 0, p=0.25 in state 1)
4. Try adding a 4th transition to state 0 or state 2
5. Explore whether WHITE trigger in state 2 could be higher (0.99)
6. Try varying the GREEN perception threshold in state 0
7. Consider whether state 1 needs a WHITE→s2 transition

### Analysis

The population is very tightly clustered (-5.88 to -6.71), all using the same topology. The champion has:
- State 0: GoToColor GREEN, p(BLACK→s1)=0.85, p(WHITE→s1)=0.75, p(sees GREEN→s1)=0.75
- State 1: GoAwayColor GREEN, p(GRAY→s0)=0.4, p(BLACK→s1)=0.85
- State 2: GoToColor RED, p(GRAY→s0)=0.8, p(BLACK→s1)=0.9, p(WHITE→s0)=0.95

Key findings this round:
- p0x2=0.75 (sees GREEN trigger) slightly better than 0.7 (champion vs rank 9/10 which have 0.7)
- p1x0=0.25 scored -6.67 (rank 2) vs p1x0=0.4 at -6.71 (rank 1) - very close, 0.4 slightly better
- p1x0=0.3 scored -6.38 (rank 5) - worse than 0.4
- Adding 4th transition to state 2 (sees RED) gave -6.60 (rank 3)
- Extra transition in state 0 (WHITE→s1 explicit) gave -6.27 (rank 3 last round)

The champion's distinguishing features vs rank 9/10 (both -6.12): p0x2=0.75 vs 0.7. This seems to help.

1. Keep champion exactly
2. Try p1x0 around 0.35-0.45 with p0x2=0.75

### Analysis

The population continues to be very tightly clustered (-5.93 to -6.71). The champion from round 6 still holds at -6.71. Round 7's best was -6.70 (essentially tied).

- p1x0=0.2 scored -6.56 (rank 4)
- p1x0=0.4, p1x1=0.8 scored -6.58 (rank 2)

- p0x2=0.8 scored -6.53 (rank 5)

The champion's parameters seem to be near optimal. The slight improvement from p1x0=0.25 is promising.

- Lower p1x0 (GRAY→return to source) seems beneficial (0.25 better than 0.4 this round)
- p1x1=0.8 might be slightly better than 0.85
- p2x0=0.85 + p2x1=0.92 promising
- Scores are very noisy - the champion (-6.71) scored -6.16 when re-evaluated with same params
- We need to reduce noise by testing key combinations multiple times

**Strategy for Round 8**:

1. Keep champion exactly

## Reasoning

### Analysis of Round 8

The new champion (-6.79) has a key difference from the previous champion: **state 1 now has 3 transitions** (not 2):
- n1x0: GRAY→s0, p=0.25 (return to source-seeking)
- n1x1: BLACK→s1, p=0.85 (stay in away-from-green)
- **n1x2: WHITE→s1, p=0.9** (new! stay in away-from-green when on white floor)

The 4-state variant also scored -6.79 (rank 2), suggesting the 3rd transition in state 1 is genuinely helpful.

**Key insights**:
- The WHITE floor transition in state 1 (p=0.9) keeping robots in "go away from green" mode when they're on white (nest area) is valuable - it means robots keep moving away from green even when they've reached the nest
- p1x0=0.25 (GRAY→s0) remains better than 0.4
- p1x0=0.15 gave -6.60 (rank 3), slightly worse
- The champion structure: s0=GoToGreen, s1=GoAwayGreen(3 transitions), s2=GoToRed(3 transitions)

**Strategy for Round 9**:
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Exploit the new 3-transition state 1 structure: vary p1x2 (WHITE→s1)
3. Try p1x2=0.8, 0.85, 0.95 
4. Try combining: p1x0=0.2 with 3 transitions in state 1
5. Try adding 4th transition to state 1 (e.g., sees RED→s2)
6. Try varying state 2 parameters
7. Try n1x2 going to s2 (GoToRed) instead of staying at s1 when on white

The WHITE trigger in s1 staying at s1 makes sense: robot arrives at nest (white floor), keeps moving away from green = actually moves into nest area and delivers. Good behavior!

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
